Primer: Searching for efﬁcient transformers for\nlangua\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 44000:47353]\nith the approximation model Mq and beam width u ≥w for γ steps. Then, we can use Mp to\ncheck all of the candidates in parallel (costing a compute budget of (w + uγ) runs of Mp). Finally, for each step, we can\naccept the guesses of Mq as long as topw(Mp) ⊆topu(Mq) to get identical results to regular beam search with Mp alone\n(with a more elaborate procedure we could also accept cases where the candidates we got happen to have higher probabilities\nthan those of Mp alone). The analysis of our method in this setting is more involved and we leave it for future work.\nA.5. Lenience\nA strong property of Algorithm 1 is that the output distribution is guaranteed to remain unchanged. That said, if we’re\nwilling to allow some changes, with nice guarantees, we can get further inference speed improvements. To further motivate\nthis, note that when we train two models with identical architectures and sizes on the same dataset, the generated probability\ndistributions will not be identical, so some lenience might make sense. Note that the results in this paper except for this\nsection use the strictest version of Algorithm 1 and don’t allow lenience of any kind.\nWe could include a lenience parameter l ∈[0, 1] and multiply q(x) by l before comparing with p(x) in Algorithm 1. This\nstill maintains the nice guarantee that no token can be sampled with probability greater than p(x)\nl . This means for example,\nthat with l =\n1\n10 no token can be sampled with more than 10X its ground truth probability, so we can guarantee that\nextremely rare tokens will remain extremely rare (there is no guarantee on the minimum probability, so lenience could hurt\nthe diversity of the samples).\nSpeciﬁcally, with a lenience factor l we have α = Ex∼q(x)\n(\n1\nlq(x) ≤p(x)\np(x)\nlq(x) lq(x) > p(x)\n= Ex∼q(x)\np(x)\nmax(p(x),lq(x)) =\nP\nx\np(x)q(x)\nmax(p(x),lq(x)) = 1\nl\nP\nx min(p(x), lq(x)) = P\nx min( p(x)\nl , q(x)).\nTable 5 shows α values for different values of l when Mp is T5-XXL (11B) and Mq is T5-small (77M). With c = 0.015,\nusing lenience values of 1, 0.5, 0.3, and 0.1 (meaning that no token can be sampled with probability greater than 1X, 2X, 3X\nand 10X of the ground truth) we get improvement factors of 2.5X, 3.1X, 3.6X, and 5X respectively.\nTable 5. α values for various values of l with standard sampling where Mp is T5-XXL (11B) on the EnDe translation task.\nMq\nl = 1\nl = 0.5\nl = 0.3\nl = 0.1\nUNIGRAM\n0.07\n0.1\n0.11\n0.16\nBIGRAM\n0.19\n0.23\n0.25\n0.32\nT5-SMALL (77M)\n0.62\n0.71\n0.76\n0.84\nT5-BASE (250M)\n0.68\n0.8\n0.83\n0.90\n12\n\n\n--- PAGE 13 ---\nFast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding\nNote that when using temperature = 0 (i.e. argmax sampling), we can no longer use lenience as above. Instead, we could\nallow some lenience before standardizing the distributions. For example, we could accept the token x sampled from Mq in\ncase p(x) ≤l · max(p). In this case, we measure similar empirical increases in α values to those with temperature = 1. For\nexample, when using lenience values of 1, 0.5, 0.3, and 0.1 for Mp T5-XXL Mq T5-small for English-German translation,\nwe get α values of 0.75, 0.75, 0.8, 0.87. Taking for example c = 0.015 and γ = 8 we get speed improvement factors of\n3.3X, 3.3X, 3.9X, and 4.9X respectively7.\n7In this case, unlike in the standard sampling case shown in Table 5, a lenience factor of 0.5 doesn’t improve the speed-up.\n13\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 32000], [36000, 39030], [44000, 47353]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 47353, "selected_characters": 38383, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-KIVI/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "399fb46e139f4cda57cbcd18c7b75fb1ddeb7dd6957d4a059efe8e40081438f3", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:14384]\n=== PDF page 1 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nZirui Liu * 1 Jiayi Yuan * 1 Hongye Jin 2 Shaochen Zhong 1\nZhaozhuo Xu 3 Vladimir Braverman 1 Beidi Chen 4 Xia Hu 1\nAbstract\nEfficiently serving large language models (LLMs)\nrequires batching many requests together to re-\nduce the cost per request. Yet, the key-value (KV)\ncache, which stores attention keys and values\nto avoid re-computations, significantly increases\nmemory demands and becomes the new bottle-\nneck in speed and memory usage. This mem-\nory demand increases with larger batch sizes and\nlonger context lengths. Additionally, the infer-\nence speed is limited by the size of KV cache,\nas the GPU’s SRAM must load the entire KV\ncache from the main GPU memory for each to-\nken generated, causing the computational core\nto be idle during this process. A straightforward\nand effective solution to reduce KV cache size\nis quantization, which decreases the total bytes\ntaken by KV cache. However, there is a lack of\nin-depth studies that explore the element distri-\nbution of KV cache to understand the hardness\nand limitation of KV cache quantization. To fill\nthe gap, we conducted a comprehensive study on\nthe element distribution in KV cache of popular\nLLMs. Our findings indicate that the key cache\nshould be quantized per-channel, i.e., group el-\nements along the channel dimension and quan-\ntize them together. In contrast, the value cache\nshould be quantized per-token. From this anal-\nysis, we developed a tuning-free 2bit KV cache\nquantization algorithm, named KIVI. With the\nhardware-friendly implementation, KIVIcan en-\nable Llama (Llama-2), Falcon, and Mistral mod-\nels to maintain almost the same quality while us-\ning 2.6× less peak memory usage (including the\nmodel weight). This reduction in memory us-\nage enables up to 4× larger batch size, bringing\n*Equal contribution. The order of authors is determined by\nflipping a coin.\n1Department of Computer Science, Rice Uni-\nversity 2Department of Computer Science, Texas A&M Uni-\nversity 3Department of Computer Science, Stevens Institute of\nTechnology 4Department of Electrical and Computer Engineer-\ning, Carnegie Mellon University. Correspondence to: Zirui Liu\n<zl105@rice.edu>, Jiayi Yuan <jy101@rice.edu>.\nPreliminary work.\n2.35× ∼3.47× throughput on real LLM infer-\nence workload. The source code is available at\nhttps://github.com/jy-yuan/KIVI.\n1. Introduction\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong\nperformance across a wide range of tasks (Brown et al.,\n2020; Yuan et al., 2023; Chuang et al., 2023). However,\ntheir deployment is very costly, requiring a large number of\nhardware accelerators such as GPUs. Given these substan-\ntial costs, one natural way to reduce the cost per request is to\ncombine a sufficient number of requests together for batch\nprocessing. However, in this batch inference scenario, the\nkey-value cache (KV cache), which holds the attention keys\nand values during generation to prevent re-computations, is\nbecoming the new memory and speed bottleneck. This bot-\ntleneck becomes more pronounced with larger batch sizes\nand longer context lengths. For instance, in 540B PaLM,\nwith a batch size of 512 and a context length of 2048, KV\ncache alone can take 3TB. This is 3 times the size of the\nmodel’s parameters (Pope et al., 2023). Also, the GPU\nSRAM has to load the whole KV cache from the GPU de-\nvice memory for every token generated, during which the\ncomputational cores are idle. Thus, reducing KV cache size\nin LLMs while maintaining accuracy is important.\nExisting works towards this problem can be roughly divided\ninto three categories. First, some work suggests reducing\nthe number of heads in KV cache, such as multi-query\nattention (Shazeer, 2019) and multi-group attention (Ainslie\net al., 2023). However, these methods require either training\nthe model from scratch or fine-tuning the existing model.\nSecond, another research line reduces KV cache size by\nevicting unimportant tokens (Zhang et al., 2023). Third,\nsome other works try to solve this problem from the system\nperspective, e.g., offloading KV cache (Sheng et al., 2023)\nor extending virtual memory and paging techniques into the\nattention mechanism (Kwon et al., 2023a).\nTo reduce the size of KV cache, the most simple and ef-\nfective way is to reduce the total bytes taken by KV cache,\nnamely, quantization. Unlike the well-studied weight quan-\ntization (Lin et al., 2023; Xiao et al., 2023a), to the best of\nour knowledge, only a few studies applied the vanilla 4bit\n1\narXiv:2402.02750v1  [cs.CL]  5 Feb 2024\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nper-token\nquantization\nper-channel\nquantization\nFigure 1: Definition of per-token and per-channel quantiza-\ntion. X ∈Rlprompt×d is key/value cache where lprompt is the\nnumber of tokens and d is the number of channels. zX is\nthe zero-point, sX is the scaling factor.\nround-to-nearest quantization to KV cache (Sheng et al.,\n2023; Zhang et al., 2023) due to the streaming nature of KV\ncache. There is a lack of in-depth studies that explore the\nelement distribution of KV cache to understand the hardness\nand limitation of KV cache quantization.\nTo fill the gap, we study the element distribution of KV\ncache. Our analysis suggests:\n• For the key cache, there are a few fixed channels whose\nmagnitudes are very large, which is consistent with\nprevious finding (Lin et al., 2023; Xiao et al., 2023a).\nThus, as shown in Figure 1 right, key cache should be\nquantized per-channel, i.e., group elements along the\nchannel dimension and quantize them together. In this\nway, it can confine the error to each individual channel,\nwithout impacting the other normal channels.\n• For the value cache, there is no obvious outlier pat-\ntern. Although value cache has no obvious outlier\npattern, we experimentally show that it can only be\nquantized per-token because it is used to calculate the\nattention output, which is essentially a value cache\nmixer. As shown in Figure 1 left, the per-token quan-\ntization can confine the error inside each individual\ntoken and ensure that the quantization of one token\ndoes not adversely impact the others.\nBased on the above insights, we propose KIVI, a plug-and-\nplay extreme low-bit KV cache quantization method. KIVI\nquantizes key cache per-channel and quantizes value cache\nper-token. The per-token value cache quantization aligns\nwell with the streaming nature of auto-regressive inference,\nallowing newly quantized tensors to be directly appended\nto the existing quantized value cache by token dimension.\nHowever, for per-channel key cache quantization, the quan-\ntization process spans different tokens, which cannot be\ndirectly implemented in this streaming setting. Since the\nnumber of tokens in key cache can be arbitrary, our key\nidea is to split key cache into two parts. The first part is\nthe grouped key cache, which contains several groups of\ntokens and each group has a certain number of tokens. The\nsecond part is the residual key cache, which does not have\na sufficient number of tokens to form a complete group.\nSimilarly, we split value cache into the grouped and residual\nparts to maintain the model performance. We only apply\ngroup-wise quantization to the grouped key cache and value\ncache, while the residual key cache and value cache are kept\nin full precision. The grouped and residual parts can be\ncombined when computing the attention score using tiled\nmatrix multiplication. Our contributions are summarized as\nfollows:\n• Extensive analysis regarding the outlier patterns\nand quantization error of KV cache in commonly-\nused LLMs. Our observations suggest that key cache\nshould be quantized per-channel and value cache\nshould be quantized per-token. We also explain in-\ndepth why these caches require different quantization\napproaches.\n• A new plug-and-play 2bit KV cache quantization\nalgorithm without any fine-tuning, KIVI, with\nhardware-friendly implementation. We conduct an\nextensive evaluation for KIVIwith Llama (Llama-2),\nMistral, and Falcon on popular generation tasks. KIVI\ncan efficiently compress KV cache to 2bit and bring\n2.6× peak memory usage reduction for Llama-2-7B,\nwith little to no accuracy drop. With our efficient\nsystem implementation, this memory reduction in re-\nturn enables up to 4× larger batch size and brings\n2.35× ∼3.47× throughput.\n2. Background: Attention Inference-Time\nWorkflow\nThe LLM attention inference-time workflow involves two\nphases: i) the prefill phase, where the input prompt is used to\ngenerate KV cache for each transformer layer of LLMs; and\nii) the decoding phase, where the model uses and updates\nKV cache to generate the next token, one at a time.\nPrefill Phase.\nLet X ∈Rb×lprompt×d be the input tensor,\nwhere b is the batch size, lprompt is the length of the input\nprompt, and d is the model hidden size. For convenience,\nwe ignore the layer index here. The key, value tensors can\nbe computed by\nXK = XWK, XV = XWV ,\nwhere WK, WV ∈Rd×d are the key and value layer weight,\nrespectively. After obtaining XK and XV , they are cached\nin the memory for the ease of decoding.\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nDecoding Phase.\nLet t ∈Rb×1×d be the current input\ntoken embedding. Let tK = tWK and tV = tWV be the\nkey and value layer output, respectively. We first update KV\ncache:\nXK ←Concat(XK, tK),\nXV ←Concat(XV , tV ),\nthen calculate the attention output as:\ntQ = tWQ,\nA = Softmax(tQX⊤\nK),\ntO = AXV ,\n(1)\nwhere WQ is the weight matrix of the query layer. For ease\nof illustration, we ignore the attention output layer and the\nother parts of the inference workflow.\nMemory and Speed Analysis.\nThe above process is re-\npeated until a special token indicating the sentence’s con-\nclusion is reached. Let lgen be the number of generated\ntokens. From the above analysis, the shape of KV cache is\nb × (lprompt + lgen) × d. To get a sense of the scale, consider\nthe OPT-175B model with a batch size b 512, a prompt\nlength lprompt 512, and an output length lgen 32. The KV\ncache requires 1.2TB, which is 3.8 times the model weights\n(Sheng et al., 2023). Besides the memory, the inference\nspeed is also decided by the KV cache size. The GPU needs\nto load KV cache from GPU main memory to GPU SRAM\nonce for every token generated during which the computa-\ntional core of the chip is essentially idle (Pope et al., 2023;\nKwon et al., 2023a).\n3. Methodology\nIn scenarios with long contexts or batched inferences, the\nmemory and speed bottlenecks are storing and loading KV\ncache size. The most simple and effective way to alleviate\nthis problem is to reduce the total bytes occupied by KV\ncache, specifically, quantization. Following this motivation,\nwe first evaluate the performance of the existing quantiza-\ntion method in Section 3.1. Our observations suggest that\nkey and value cache should be quantized along different\ndimensions. We analyze the rationale behind this observa-\ntion in Section 3.2. Then based on the analysis, we propose\nKIVI, a new KV cache quantization method along with its\nstreaming data structure, detailed in Section 3.3.\n3.1. Preliminary Study of KV Cache Quantization\nAs we analyzed Section 2, KV cache functions as a stream-\ning data structure, where the new tensor arrives sequentially.\nThus, optimization-based methods like GPTQ (Frantar et al.,\n2022) are unsuitable for quantizing KV cache due to the\nTable 1: The results of simulated KV cache group-wise\nquantization with various configurations. The group size\nis set as 32. C stands for per-channel quantization and T\nstands for per-token quantization. Please check the whole\nevaluation in Table 3.\nLlama-2-13B\nCoQA\nTruthfulQA\n16bit\n66.37\n29.53\n4bit (K - T, V - T)\n66.48\n29.51\n2bit (K - T, V - T)\n52.93\n24.98\n2bit (K - C, V - C)\n2.88\n0.74\n2bit (K - T, V - C)\n2.80\n0.26\n2bit (K - C, V - T)\n63.53\n28.60\noverhead. To the best of our knowledge, the most flexible\nway for quantizing KV cache is the round-to-nearest quan-\ntization. The B−bit integer quantization-dequantization\nprocess can be expressed as:\nQ(X) = ⌊X −zX\nsX\n⌉,\nX′ = Q(X) · sX + zX,\nwhere zX = min X is the zero-point, sX = (max X −\nmin X)/(2B −1) is the scaling factor, and ⌊·⌉is the round-\ning operation. Here we ignore the batch size for ease of\nunderstanding. As shown in Figure 1, X is quantized along\neither the token or channel dimension group-wisely.\nConsidering the streaming nature of KV cache, previous\nstudies often apply per-token quantization to both key and\nvalue cache since the newly quantized KV cache can be\nnaively added to the existing quantized one along the token\ndimension (Sheng et al., 2023). While per-channel quanti-\nzation is non-trivial, we have designed a padding method to\nimplement per-channel quantization to explore its effect on\nboth key and value cache.\nSetting.\nIn Table 1, we show the results of fake KV cache\ngroup-wise quantization with different configurations on the\nLlama-2-13B model for the CoQA and TruthfulQA tasks.\nWe use a group size of 32 for all configurations. Here fake\nquantization means we simulate the quantization process\nby first quantizing KV cache into lower precision and then\ndequantizing it in the attention layer. For per-channel quan-\ntization, if the number of tokens is not divided evenly into\ngroups, we add zero-padding to ensure it can be grouped\nperfectly. In this way, we ensure that all tokens in KV cache\nare quantized for a fair comparison. The detailed experi-\nmental setting can be found in Section 4.1. Specifically, we\nobserve that:\nOB 1. When using the commonly used per-token quanti-\nzation to both key and value caches, INT4 precision can\nmaintain accuracy. However, reducing it to INT2 results in\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\n0\n50 100 150 200\nToken\n0\n25\n50\n75\n100\n125\nChannel\n0.0\n2.5\n5.0\n7.5\n10.0\n12.5\nAbsolute Value\nLlama-2-13B Layer 16\nHead 0 Key Cache\n0\n50 100 150 200\nToken\n0\n25\n50\n75\n100\n125\nChannel\n0.5\n1.0\n1.5\n2.0\nAbsolute Value\nLlama-2-13B Layer 16\nHead 0 Value Cache\n0\n50 100 150 200\nToken\n0\n25\n50\n75\n100\n125\nChannel\n0.0\n2.5\n5.0\n7.5\n10.0\n12.5\nLlama-2-13B Layer 31\nHead 0 Key Cache\n0\n50 100 150 200\nToken\n0\n25\n50\n75\n100\n125\nChannel\n0.5\n1.0\n1.5\n2.0\n2.5\nLlama-2-13B Layer 31\nHead 0 Value Cache\n0\n50 100 150 200\nToken\n0\n20\n40\n60\nChannel\n0\n2\n4\n6\n8\n10\nAbsolute Value\nFalcon-7B Layer 16\nHead \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 16000:24000]\ntion can con-\nfine the quantization error to each individual channel, with-\nout impacting the other normal channels. For ease of illustration, we omit the value cache and attention output parts. The\ndetailed pseudo-code is provided in Algorithm 1. Here “Q_Matmul” is the mix-precision matrix multiplication which fuses\nthe dequantization with matrix multiplication at the tiling level.\nFigure 2 alone cannot explain OB2, which indicates value\ncache should only be quantized per-token. This is because\nFigure 2 implies that errors should be comparable for both\nper-token and per-channel quantization, given the absence\nof a clear pattern. As shown in Equation (1), value cache\nis used to calculate the attention output tO. Instead of\nanalyzing the quantization error of value cache XV , in\nTable 2 we analyze the relative error ∆= ∥AXV −AX′\nV\nAXV\n∥F\nwith different quantization configurations. Surprisingly, we\nobserve that the per-token quantization error is almost 15×\nsmaller than per-channel quantization, which explains why\nOB2 happens. The intuition behind this observation stems\nfrom the attention sparsity. Equation (1) can be written as:\n[AXV ]i∗=\nlprompt\nX\nj=1\nAij[XV ]j∗,\n(2)\nwhere [XV ]j∗is the j-th row of XV . From Equation (2),\nthe attention output is the weighted summation of value\ncache across various tokens, with the weights being the\nattention scores. Since the attention score is highly sparse\n(Tian et al., 2023), the output is just the combination of\nvalue caches of a few important tokens. The per-token\nquantization can confine the error to each individual token.\nThus, quantizing other tokens does not affect the accuracy\nof important tokens. Thus, per-token quantization leads to a\nmuch smaller relative error ∆.\n3.3. KIVI: Algorithm and System Support\nAlgorithm.\nAs we previously analyzed, key cache should\nbe quantized per-channel and value cache should be quan-\ntized per-token. Recall that key and value cache of newly\ngenerated tokens arrive sequentially. From the implemen-\ntation perspective, per-token quantization aligns well with\nstreaming settings, allowing newly quantized tensors to be\ndirectly appended to the existing quantized value cache\nby token dimension. However, for per-channel quantiza-\ntion, the quantization process spans across different tokens,\nwhich cannot be directly implemented in the streaming set-\nting. As shown in Figure 3, our key idea to solve this prob-\nlem is to group key cache every G tokens and quantize them\nseparately. Because the number of tokens in XK can be ar-\nbitrary, we split XK into two parts, namely, the grouped part\nXKg = XK[: l −r] and residual part XKr = XK[l −r :],\nwhere l is the number of tokens inside the current key cache\nXK, r is the number of residual tokens, where l −r can be\ndivisible by G.\nSince XKg can be evenly divided into (l −r)/G groups,\nwe only store Q(XKg) with group-wise quantization, while\nXKr is kept in full precision. During the decoding process,\neach newly arrived key cache tK is added to XKr and once\nXKr reaches R tokens, which is a hyperparameter - residual\nlength, we quantize and concatenate it with the previously\nquantized Q(XKG). Then we reset XKr to an empty tensor.\nWe note that R should be divisible by G. With tiled matrix\nmultiplication, the raw attention logits is then calculated as:\nAg = tQQ(X⊤\nKg),\nXKr = Concat([XKr, tK]),\nAr = tQX⊤\nKr,\nA = Concat([Ag, Ar]).\n(3)\nFor value cache, similar to key cache, we also split it into\ntwo parts and keep the most recent value cache in full pre-\ncision, namely, XVg and XVr. Specifically, we maintain a\nqueue and each newly arrived value cache is pushed into\nthe queue. Once the queue reaches the predefined residual\nlength R, the most outdated value cache is poped. Then the\npoped value cache is quantized per-token and concatenated\nwith the previously quantized value cache along the token\ndimension.\nAs shown in Figure 3, we also emphasize that during the\nprefill phase, the exact key and value tensors are passed to\nthe next layers, although only the quantized KV cache is\nretained in memory. The whole algorithm can be found in\nAppendix A Algorithm 1.\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nAnalysis.\nIn KIVI, the grouped key cache XKg and value\ncache XVg is quantized, while the residual key cache XKr\nand value cache XVr is kept in full precision. By design,\nthere are at most R tokens inside XKr or XVr. In practice,\nwe set R ≤128 and the sequence length lprompt + lgen is\noften much longer than R. Thus the memory overhead from\nXKr and XVr is negligible when considering the benefit\nfrom extreme low-bit quantization, especially for the long\ncontext scenarios. Also, since the newly arrived key and\nvalue tensors are added to XKr and XVr in full precision,\nKIVImaintains a full precision KV cache sliding window\nfor the local relevant tokens. This window size is expected\nto be R\n2 for key cache, and R for value cache. Later in the\nexperiment section, we show that this full precision sliding\nwindow is crucial for obtaining desirable performance on\nhard tasks, such as GSM8K.\nSystem Support.\nWe provide a hardware-friendly imple-\nmentation for running KIVI on GPUs. To minimize the\noverhead, we have fused the dequantization process with\nmatrix multiplication, e.g., Q_MatMul in Figure 3, using\nCUDA. We also implement the group-wise quantization ker-\nnel in Triton. Our method is fully compatible with weight-\nonly quantization.\n4. Experiments\n4.1. Settings\nModels.\nWe evaluate KIVI using two popular recent\nmodel families: Llama/Llama-2 (Touvron et al., 2023a;b)),\nFalcon (Penedo et al., 2023) and Mistral (Jiang et al., 2023).\nWe note that the Llama and Mistral model is based on multi-\nhead attention, while Falcon is based on multi-query atten-\ntion (Shazeer, 2019). We use the Hugging Face Transform-\ners codebase and implement the KIVIalgorithm based on\nit. Following previous work (Sheng et al., 2023), the group\nsize G in Algorithm 1 for quantization is set as 32 across all\nexperiments, the residual length R for key and value cache\nis set to 128.\nTasks.\nAs we analyzed in Section 2, the KV cache size\ngrows larger with longer context. Thus we evaluate KIVI\nunder the normal context length and long context setting,\nrespectively. Specifically, we adopt generation tasks from\nLM-Eval (Gao et al., 2021) for normal context length eval-\nuation and LongBench (Bai et al., 2023) for long context\nevaluation, respectively1. For LM-eval, we adopt CoQA\n(Exact match accuracy), TruthfulQA (BLEU score), and\nGSM8K (Exact match accuracy). For LongBench, we chose\n1The closed-end tasks such as MMLU are not ideal to evaluate\nKIVIsince they only involve one decoding step and directly fetch\nthe output logits, which is not suitable for studying the impact of\ncompressed KV cache.\ntasks from four subgroups. Specifically, Qasper (F1 score)\nis a Single-Docu\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 28000:44000]\napply KIVIover Llama2-7B, Llama2-13B, Llama2-\n7B-Chat, Llama2-13B-Chat, Falcon-7B and Mistral-7B. Ta-\nble 4 suggests that KIVI is an effective method for KV\ncache compression with minimal impact on accuracy across\nvarious hard long context generation tasks.\n4.2.3. ABLATION\nIn this section, we benchmark KIVI on GSM8K, one of\nthe hardest generation tasks, to show the effect of hyperpa-\nrameters group size G and residual length R on the model\nperformance. For full results of KIVIwith a residual length\nof 32, please refer to Appendix B.\nThe effect of group size. We fix the residual length at 128\nand vary the group sizes to 32, 64, and 128. From Table 5,\nwe observe that group sizes 32 and 64 yield similar results,\nwhereas the performance significantly decreases when the\ngroup size reaches 128.\nFigure 4: Memory usage and throughput comparison be-\ntween 2bit KIVI and 16bit baseline. KIVI can achieve\nhigher throughput by enabling a larger batch size.\nThe effect of residual length. We fix the group size at 32\nand vary the residual length across 32, 64, 96, and 128. As\nshown in Table 5, there is no consistent pattern between\nresidual lengths and model accuracy. Namely, while a resid-\nual length of 128 achieves good results, 32 and 96 yield\nsimilar outcomes, but a residual length of 64 results in the\nworst performance.\n4.2.4. EFFICIENCY COMPARISON\nTo evaluate the wall-clock time efficiency of KIVI, follow-\ning vLLM (Kwon et al., 2023a), we synthesize workloads\nbased on ShareGPT (sha, 2023), which contain input and\noutput texts of real LLM services. In average, the dataset\nfeatures an input prompt length lprompt of 161 and an output\nlength lgen of 338 (Kwon et al., 2023a). We increase the\nbatch size until out of memory and report the peak memory\nusage and throughput between KIVI(with residual length\n32 and 128) and FP16 baseline for the Llama-2-7B model.\nThe hardware here is a single NVIDIA A100 GPU (80GB).\nAs shown in Figure 4, with similar maximum memory us-\nage, KIVI enables up to 4× larger batch size and gives\n2.35× ∼3.47× larger throughput. This throughput num-\nber can grow larger with longer context length and output\nlength. We also note that this speedup can be greatly in-\ncreased if we further fuse the KV cache quantization\nprocess with previous operations. We leave it as one of\nthe future work.\n7\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nTable 4: Performance evaluation of KIVIon various models across a range of benchmarks in LongBench. Related Work\nQuantization techniques have been widely applied in Large\nLanguage Models (Frantar et al., 2022; Lin et al., 2023;\nKim et al., 2023; Xu et al., 2023). A main branch of LLM\nquantization is weight-only quantization, which involves\nthe quantization of model weights to lower precision. For\ninstance, AWQ (Lin et al., 2023) cleverly quantizes model\nweights to INT4 and INT3 using an activation-aware manner.\nGPTQ (Frantar et al., 2022) utilizes approximate second-\norder information to quantize model weights both accurately\nand efficiently. SqueezeLLM (Kim et al., 2023) adopts the\nconcept of sensitivity-based non-uniform quantization along\nwith Dense-and-Sparse decomposition. This line of work is\northogonal to ours, as they can be combined together.\nSmoothQuant (Xiao et al., 2023a) is a post-training quan-\ntization method that is more closely related to our work.\nThis method uses equivalent transformations to balance\nthe quantization complexity for both activation and weight,\nmaking the activation easier to quantize. SmoothQuant can\ncompress KV cache to 8bit with minor performance loss.\nHowever, it faces a significant accuracy drop when scaled\ndown to 4bit or less.\nvLLM (Kwon et al., 2023b) and S3 (Jin et al., 2023) are\nsystem-level works, which include memory management\nthrough the use of PagedAttention or memory usage predic-\ntion. These approaches can lower the memory requirements\nof KV cache and simultaneously increase model throughput.\nThis research direction is orthogonal to our work, as such\nsystem-level optimizations can also be applied to enhance\nour algorithm.\nSeveral other works also consider compressing KV cache\nby evicting unimportant tokens. H2O (Zhang et al., 2023)\nretains only a small portion of tokens that contribute signifi-\ncantly to the attention scores. StreamingLLM (Xiao et al.,\n2023b) is based on the observation of “attention sink” and\nmaintains only a few initial tokens to preserve performance.\nUnlike these works, our KIVIretains all input tokens and\ncompresses them into lower precision. This line of work is\northogonal to ours, as they also can be combined together.\n6. Conclusion and Future Work\nIn this paper, we systematically analyze KV cache element\ndistribution in popular LLMs. We conclude that key cache\nshould be quantized per-channel and value cache should\nbe quantized per token. Based on these observations, we\npropose KIVI, a plug-and-play 2bit KV cache quantization\nalgorithm without the need for any tuning. In real LLM\nworkload, KIVI allows up to 4× larger batch sizes and\n3.47× throughput. In the future, we will further optimize\nthe implementation to reduce the overhead of quantization\nprocess during the prefill and decoding phase.\n8\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\n7. Detailed Implementations\nIn this section, we present the algorithm for KIVIas dis-\ncussed in Section 3.3. Specifically, we provide the pseu-\ndocode for KIVIwhen calculating the attention output in\nthe prefill and decoding phases.\nAlgorithm 1: The KIVIPrefill & Decoding Algorithm\nparameter: group size G, residual length R\nprocedure Prefill:\nInput: X ∈Rlprompt×d\nXK = XWK, XV = XWV\nXVg = XV [: lprompt−R], XVr = XV [lprompt−R :]\nQ(XVg) ←\nGroupQuant(XVg, dim=token, numGroup=d//G)\nQ(XKg), XKr ←KeyQuant(XK)\nKV cache ←Q(XKg), XKr, Q(XVg), XVr\nreturn XK, XV\nend\nprocedure Decoding:\nInput: KV cache, t ∈R1×d\ntQ = tWQ, tK = tWK, tV = tWV\nQ(XKg), XKr, Q(XVg), XVr ←KV cache\nXKr ←Concat([XKr, tK], dim=token)\nXVr ←Concat([XVr, tV ], dim=token)\nif len(XKr) = R then\nQ(XKr), _ ←KeyQuant(XKr)\nQ(XKg) ←\nConcat([Q(XKg), Q(XKr)], dim=token)\nXKr ←empty tensor.\nend\nif len(XVr) > R then\nQ(XV ′r ) ←GroupQuant(XVr[:\n−R], dim=token, numGroup = d//G)\nQ(XVg) ←\nConcat([Q(XVg), Q(XV ′r )], dim=token)\nXVr ←XVr[−R :]\nend\nA ←Concat([tQQ(XKg)⊤, tQX⊤\nKr], dim=token)\nAg = Softmax(A)[: −R], Ar =\nSoftmax(A)[−R :]\ntO ←AgQ(XVg) + ArXVr\nKV cache ←Q(XKg), XKr, Q(XVg), XVr\nreturn tO\nend\nfunction KeyQuant(XK ∈Rl×d):\nr = l%R,\nXKg = XK[: l −r], XKr = XK[l −r :]\nQ(XKg) ←\nGroupQuant(XKg, dim=channel, numGroup=l//G)\nreturn Q(XKg), XKr\nend\nB. More Experimental Results\nIn our efficiency evaluation, we observe that with a residual\nlength of 32, KIVIachieves a significantly higher memory\ncompression rate, which in turn leads to increased through-\nput. Additionally, our ablation study reveals that changing\nthe residual length from 128 to 32 does not result in a sub-\nstantial performance gap. We demonstrate KIVI with a\nresidual length of 32 across all benchmark datasets. As\nshown in Tables 6 and 7, KIVIwith a residual length of 32\nalso delivers performance comparable to that of the 16-bit\nfull model.\nTable 6: Performance comparison between 16bit, KIVI-2\n(2bit) / KIVI-4 (4bit) with residual length 128 and 32\nacross various models. R32 stands for residual length 32.\nModel\nQasper\nQMSum\nMultiNews\nTREC\nTriviaQA\nSAMSum\nLCC\nRepoBench-P\nAverage\nLlama2-7B\n16bit\n9.52\n21.28\n3.51\n66\n87.72\n41.69\n66.66\n59.82\n44.52\nKIVI-2 R128\n9.31\n20.5\n1.14\n66\n87.42\n42.71\n66.88\n60.23\n44.27\nKIVI-2 R32\n9.26\n20.53\n0.97\n66\n87.42\n42.61\n66.22\n59.67\n44.08\nLlama2-13B\n16bit\n9.32\n21.38\n3.71\n70\n87.87\n43.55\n66.61\n56.42\n44.85\nKIVI-2 R128\n8.58\n20.69\n6.19\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 14384], [16000, 24000], [28000, 44000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 44977, "selected_characters": 38384, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-TORCH-CUDA/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "4756fc37be9c52d4128b5f4b46f996fddb8b136a9ef51183a6f0e1c7d49b18df", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 16000:20000]\nmory.\n\n.. _bwd-cuda-stream-semantics:\n\nStream semantics of backward passes\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nEach backward CUDA op runs on the same stream that was used for its corresponding forward op.\nIf your forward pass runs independent ops in parallel on different streams,\nthis helps the backward pass exploit that same parallelism.\n\nThe stream semantics of a backward call with respect to surrounding ops are the same\nas for any other call. The backward pass inserts internal syncs to ensure this even when\nbackward ops run on multiple streams as described in the previous paragraph.\nMore concretely, when calling\n:func:`autograd.backward<torch.autograd.backward>`,\n:func:`autograd.grad<torch.autograd.grad>`, or\n:meth:`tensor.backward<torch.Tensor.backward>`,\nand optionally supplying CUDA tensor(s) as the  initial gradient(s) (e.g.,\n:func:`autograd.backward(..., grad_tensors=initial_grads)<torch.autograd.backward>`,\n:func:`autograd.grad(..., grad_outputs=initial_grads)<torch.autograd.grad>`, or\n:meth:`tensor.backward(..., gradient=initial_grad)<torch.Tensor.backward>`),\nthe acts of\n\n1. optionally populating initial gradient(s),\n2. invoking the backward pass, and\n3. using the gradients\n\nhave the same stream-semantics relationship as any group of ops::\n\n    s = torch.cuda.Stream()\n\n    # Safe, grads are used in the same stream context as backward()\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        loss.backward()\n        use grads\n\n    # Unsafe\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        loss.backward()\n    use grads\n\n    # Safe, with synchronization\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        loss.backward()\n    torch.cuda.current_stream().wait_stream(s)\n    use grads\n\n    # Safe, populating initial grad and invoking backward are in the same stream context\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        loss.backward(gradient=torch.ones_like(loss))\n\n    # Unsafe, populating initial_grad and invoking backward are in different stream contexts,\n    # without synchronization\n    initial_grad = torch.ones_like(loss)\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        loss.backward(gradient=initial_grad)\n\n    # Safe, with synchronization\n    initial_grad = torch.ones_like(loss)\n    s.wait_stream(torch.cuda.current_stream())\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        initial_grad.record_stream(s)\n        loss.backward(gradient=initial_grad)\n\nBC note: Using grads on the default stream\n~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\nIn prior versions of PyTorch (1.9 and earlier), the autograd engine always synced\nthe default stream with all backward ops, so the following pattern::\n\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        loss.backward()\n    use grads\n\nwas safe as long as ``use grads`` happened on the default stream.\nIn present PyTorch, that pattern is no longer safe. If ``backward()``\nand ``use grads`` are in different stream contexts, you must sync the streams::\n\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        loss.backward()\n    torch.cuda.current_stream().wait_stream(s)\n    use grads\n\neven if ``use grads`` is on the default stream.\n\n.. _CUDA stream: https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-c-programming-guide/index.html#streams\n\n.. _cuda-memory-management:\n\nMemory management\n-----------------\n\nPyTorch uses a caching memory allocator to speed up memory allocations. This\nallows fast memory deallocation without device synchronizations. However, the\nunused memory managed by the allocator will still show as if used in\n``nvidia-smi``. You can use :meth:`~torch.cuda.memory_allocated` and\n:meth:`~torch.cuda.max_memory_allocated` to monitor memory occupied by\ntensors, and use :meth:`~torch.cuda.memory_reserved` and\n:meth:`~torch.cuda.max_memory_reserved` to monitor the total amount of memory\nmanaged by the caching allocator. Calling :meth:`~torch.cuda.empty_cache`\nreleases all **unused** cached memory from PyTorch so that those can be used\nby other GPU applications. However, the occupied GPU memory by tensors will not\nbe freed so it can not increase the amount of GPU memory available for PyTorch.\n\nTo better \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 28000:36000]\nn is a boolean flag that determines whether to\n  use the CUDA API's cudaHostRegister function for allocating pinned memory instead\n  of the default cudaHostAlloc. When set to True, the memory is allocated using regular\n  malloc and then pages are mapped to the memory before calling cudaHostRegister.\n  This pre-mapping of pages helps reduce the lock time during the execution\n  of cudaHostRegister.\n\n* `pinned_num_register_threads` option is only valid when pinned_use_cuda_host_register\n  is set to True. By default, one thread is used to map the pages. This option allows\n  using more threads to parallelize the page mapping operations to reduce the overall\n  allocation time of pinned memory. A good value for this option is 8 based on\n  benchmarking results.\n\n* `pinned_use_background_threads` option is a boolean flag to enable background thread\n  for processing events. This avoids any slow path associated with querying/processing of\n  events in the fast allocation path. This feature is disabled by default.\n\n* `pinned_reserve_segment_size_mb` option is a size in MB to reserve for pinned memory\n  segment. This allocates a large segment of pinned memory upfront and then uses to allocate\n  small size requests. This helps reduce the number of expensive device library calls.\n\n* `pinned_max_round_threshold_mb` option sets the maximum allocation size (in MB)\n  that will be rounded up to the nearest power-of-2. Allocations larger than this threshold\n  will use their exact requested size, which can reduce memory waste for large pinned memory\n  allocations. For example, with a threshold of 128 MB, a 129 MB allocation will use 129 MB\n  instead of being rounded to 256 MB. By default, this option is disabled.\n  Allocations that exceed `pinned_max_cached_size_mb` are never rounded up, regardless\n  of this setting, since rounding is only useful for cached blocks.\n\n* `pinned_max_cached_size_mb` option sets the maximum block size (in MB) that will be cached\n  in the free list for reuse. Blocks larger than this threshold will not be rounded up to\n  power-of-2 and will be freed immediately when no longer in use, rather than being cached.\n  This can help reduce peak memory usage for workloads with large pinned memory allocations\n  that are used infrequently. By default, this option is disabled (all blocks are cached).\n\n* ``graph_capture_record_stream_reuse`` (experimental, default: `False`)\n  If set to `True`, the CUDA caching allocator will attempt to reclaim device memory during\n  CUDA Graph capture by using the graph topology (instead of CUDA events) to determine\n  when a freed block is safe to reuse. This can reduce peak memory during long captures that free\n  and reallocate buffers across multiple streams, especially when the capture DAG frequently\n  reaches joined frontiers.\n\n* ``per_process_memory_fraction`` option limits the amount of memory that can be allocated\n  on all the CUDA devices to a specified fraction of the available memory. This is a value\n  between 0 and 1. Attempting to allocate more memory will raise an out of memory error.\n\n* ``throw_on_cudamalloc_oom`` (default: ``False``)\n  When set to ``True``, the allocator will preemptively reject allocations that would\n  exceed the ``per_process_memory_fraction`` limit by throwing an ``OutOfMemoryError``\n  instead of attempting the allocation through the driver, which could trigger a fatal\n  GPU runtime abort (e.g., ``HSA_STATUS_ERROR_OUT_OF_RESOURCES`` on ROCm). The rejected\n  allocation skips the retry chain and throws immediately, allowing the caller to catch\n  the exception and handle it gracefully.\n\n  Example configuration:\n\n  .. code-block:: bash\n\n      PYTORCH_CUDA_ALLOC_CONF=per_process_memory_fraction:0.95,throw_on_cudamalloc_oom:True\n\n  This is particularly useful for inference serving, where a fatal GPU OOM would crash the\n  server process. With this option, the serving framework can catch the ``OutOfMemoryError``\n  and reject the individual request while continuing to serve subsequent requests.\n\n.. note::\n\n    Some stats reported by the\n    :ref:`CUDA memory management API<cuda-memory-management-api>`\n    are specific to ``backend:native``, and are not meaningful with\n    ``backend:cudaMallocAsync``.\n    See each function's docstring for details.\n\n.. _CUDA's built-in asynchronous allocator:\n    https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/using-cuda-stream-ordered-memory-allocator-part-1/\n\n.. _cuda-memory-custom-allocator:\n\nUsing custom memory allocators for CUDA\n---------------------------------------\n\nIt is possible to define allocators as simple functions in C/C++ and compile\nthem as a shared library, the code below shows a basic allocator that just\ntraces all the memory operations.\n\n.. code:: C++\n\n   #include <sys/types.h>\n   #include <cuda_runtime_api.h>\n   #include <iostream>\n   // Compile with g++ alloc.cc -o alloc.so -I/usr/local/cuda/include -shared -fPIC\n   extern \"C\" {\n   void* my_malloc(ssize_t size, int device, cudaStream_t stream) {\n      void *ptr;\n      cudaMalloc(&ptr, size);\n      std::cout<<\"alloc \"<<ptr<<size<<std::endl;\n      return ptr;\n   }\n\n   void my_free(void* ptr, ssize_t size, int device, cudaStream_t stream) {\n      std::cout<<\"free \"<<ptr<< \" \"<<stream<<std::endl;\n      cudaFree(ptr);\n   }\n   }\n\n\nThis can be used in python through the :class:`torch.cuda.memory.CUDAPluggableAllocator`.\nThe user is responsible for supplying the path to the `.so` file and the name\nof the alloc/free functions that match the signatures specified above.\n\n.. code:: python\n\n   import torch\n\n   # Load the allocator\n   new_alloc = torch.cuda.memory.CUDAPluggableAllocator(\n       'alloc.so', 'my_malloc', 'my_free')\n   # Swap the current allocator\n   torch.cuda.memory.change_current_allocator(new_alloc)\n   # This will allocate memory in the device using the new allocator\n   b = torch.zeros(10, device='cuda')\n\n\n.. code:: python\n\n   import torch\n\n   # Do an initial memory allocator\n   b = torch.zeros(10, device='cuda')\n   # Load the allocator\n   new_alloc = torch.cuda.memory.CUDAPluggableAllocator(\n       'alloc.so', 'my_malloc', 'my_free')\n   # This will error since the current allocator was already instantiated\n   torch.cuda.memory.change_current_allocator(new_alloc)\n\n.. cublas-workspaces:\n\nMixing different CUDA system allocators in the same program\n-----------------------------------------------------------\nDepending on your use case, :meth:`~torch.cuda.change_current_allocator` may not be what you\nwant to use, since it swaps the CUDA allocator for the entire program (similar to\n``PYTORCH_ALLOC_CONF=backend:cudaMallocAsync``). For instance, if the swapped allocator doesn't\nhave caching mechanism, you will lose all the benefits of PyTorch's CUDACachingAllocator. Instead,\nyou can selectively mark a region of PyTorch code to use a custom allocator using\n:class:`torch.cuda.MemPool`. This will let you use multiple CUDA system allocators in the same\nPyTorch program, along with most of the benefits of the CUDACachingAllocator (e.g. caching).\nUsing :class:`torch.cuda.MemPool`, you can utilize custom allocators that enable several features,\nsuch as:\n\n* Allocating output buffers for an all-reduce using ``ncclMemAlloc`` allocator can enable NVLink\n  Switch Reductions (NVLS). This can reduce contention between overlapping compute and communication\n  kernels on GPU resources (SMs, and Copy Engines), especially on tensor-parallel workloads.\n* For Grace CPU based systems, allocating host outputs buffers for an all-gather using ``cuMemCreate``\n  and specifying ``CU_MEM_LOCATION_TYPE_HOST_NUMA`` can enable Extended GPU Memory (EGM) based memory transfers\n  from source GPUs to the destination CPU. This accelerates the all-gather since the transfer\n  happens over NVLinks, which otherwise would have happened over bandwidth-limited, Network Interface\n  Card (NIC) links. Such an accelerated all-gather can in turn speed up model checkpointing.\n* If you are crafting a model and don't want to think about the optimal memory placemen\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 40000:44000]\nault) during MemPool\ncreation. If true, then the CUDACachingAllocator will be able to use memory in this pool as\na last resort instead of OOMing.\n\n.. code:: python\n\n    pool = torch.cuda.MemPool(allocator, use_on_oom=True)\n    with torch.cuda.use_mem_pool(pool):\n        a = torch.randn(40 * 1024 * 1024, dtype=torch.uint8, device=\"cuda\")\n    del a\n\n    # at the memory limit, this will succeed by using pool's memory in order to avoid the oom\n    b = torch.randn(40 * 1024 * 1024, dtype=torch.uint8, device=\"cuda\")\n\n\nThe following :meth:`torch.cuda.MemPool.use_count` and :meth:`torch.cuda.MemPool.snapshot`\nAPIs can be used for debugging purposes:\n\n.. code:: python\n\n   pool = torch.cuda.MemPool(allocator)\n\n   # pool's use count should be 1 at this point as MemPool object\n   # holds a reference\n   assert pool.use_count() == 1\n\n   nelem_1mb = 1024 * 1024 // 4\n\n   with torch.cuda.use_mem_pool(pool):\n       out_0 = torch.randn(nelem_1mb, device=\"cuda\")\n\n       # pool's use count should be 2 at this point as use_mem_pool\n       # holds a reference\n       assert pool.use_count() == 2\n\n   # pool's use count should be back to 1 at this point as use_mem_pool\n   # released its reference\n   assert pool.use_count() == 1\n\n   with torch.cuda.use_mem_pool(pool):\n       # pool should have 1 segment since we made a small allocation (1 MB)\n       # above and so the CUDACachingAllocator packed it into a 2 MB buffer\n       assert len(pool.snapshot()) == 1\n\n       out_1 = torch.randn(nelem_1mb, device=\"cuda\")\n\n       # pool should still have 1 segment since we made another small allocation\n       # (1 MB) that got packed into the existing 2 MB buffer\n       assert len(pool.snapshot()) == 1\n\n       out_2 = torch.randn(nelem_1mb, device=\"cuda\")\n\n       # pool now should have 2 segments since the CUDACachingAllocator had\n       # to make a new 2 MB buffer to accommodate out_2\n       assert len(pool.snapshot()) == 2\n\n\n.. note::\n\n   * :class:`torch.cuda.MemPool` holds a reference to the pool. When you use the\n     :class:`torch.cuda.use_mem_pool` context manager, it will also acquire another reference\n     to the pool. On exit of the context manager, it will release its reference. After that,\n     ideally it should only be tensors holding references to the pool. Once the tensors release\n     their references, the use count of the pool will be 1, reflecting that only the\n     :class:`torch.cuda.MemPool` object is holding a reference. Only at that point, can the memory\n     held by the pool be returned to the system when the pool's destructor is called using\n     ``del``.\n   * :class:`torch.cuda.MemPool` doesn't currently support ``expandable_segments`` mode of\n     CUDACachingAllocator.\n   * `NCCL has specific requirements`_ for a buffer to be compatible with NVLS reductions.\n     These requirements can be broken in a dynamic workload, for instance, the buffer being\n     sent to NCCL by the CUDACachingAllocator might be split and hence, not correctly aligned.\n     In those cases, NCCL can use a fallback algorithm instead of NVLS.\n   * Allocators like ``ncclMemAlloc`` can use more memory than requested, due to alignment\n     requirements (``CU_MULTICAST_GRANULARITY_RECOMMENDED``, ``CU_MULTICAST_GRANULARITY_MINIMUM``),\n     and can cause your workload to run out of memory.\n\n.. _NCCL has specific requirements:\n    https://docs.nvidia.com/deeplearning/nccl/user-guide/docs/usage/bufferreg.html#memory-allocator\n\n\nTuning NVLink Performance with Custom Memory Allocator on H100/H200 GPUs\n------------------------------------------------------------------------\nIn rare cases, performance of NVLink on H100/H200 GPUs can be influenced by the physical memory\nlayout of data, creating an opportunity for developers to tune their applications for optimal\nthroughput.\n\nAn example of how physical memory layout of data affects performance is when communication\nkernels issue unbalanced NVLink read/write operations. In the following figure, we can see\nthat each warp accesses memory \n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 48000:52000]\n\n      info->dptr = dptr;\n\n      // Release each redundant allocation\n      for (auto handle : handles_redundant) {\n          // std::cout << \"Here we release one redundant page (2MB)...\" << std::endl;\n          CHECK_CUDA(cuMemRelease(handle));\n      }\n\n      return cudaSuccess;\n  }\n\n  void customCudaFree(CustomAllocInfo* info) {\n      if (!info) return;\n\n      // CHECK_CUDA(cudaSetDevice(info->device_id));\n\n      CHECK_CUDA(cuMemUnmap(info->dptr, info->padded_size));\n\n      // Unmap and release each allocation\n      for (auto handle : info->handles) {\n          CHECK_CUDA(cuMemRelease(handle));\n      }\n\n      // Unreserve the virtual address space\n      // CHECK_CUDA(cuMemAddressFree((CUdeviceptr)*info->devPtr, info->padded_size));\n      CHECK_CUDA(cuMemAddressFree(info->dptr, info->padded_size));\n  }\n\n\ncuBLAS workspaces\n-----------------\n\nFor each combination of cuBLAS handle and CUDA stream, a cuBLAS workspace will be allocated\nif that handle and stream combination executes a cuBLAS kernel that requires a workspace.\nIn order to avoid repeatedly allocating workspaces, these workspaces are not deallocated unless\n``torch._C._cuda_clearCublasWorkspaces()`` is called. The workspace size per allocation can be\nspecified via the environment variable ``CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG`` with the format ``:[SIZE]:[COUNT]``.\nAs an example, the default workspace size per allocation is ``CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG=:4096:2:16:8``\nwhich specifies a total size of ``2 * 4096 + 8 * 16 KiB``. To force cuBLAS to avoid using workspaces,\nset ``CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG=:0:0``.\n\n.. _cufft-plan-cache:\n\ncuFFT plan cache\n----------------\n\nFor each CUDA device, an LRU cache of cuFFT plans is used to speed up repeatedly\nrunning FFT methods (e.g., :func:`torch.fft.fft`) on CUDA tensors of same geometry\nwith same configuration. Because some cuFFT plans may allocate GPU memory,\nthese caches have a maximum capacity.\n\nYou may control and query the properties of the cache of current device with\nthe following APIs:\n\n* ``torch.backends.cuda.cufft_plan_cache.max_size`` gives the capacity of the\n  cache (default is 4096 on CUDA 10 and newer, and 1023 on older CUDA versions).\n  Setting this value directly modifies the capacity.\n\n* ``torch.backends.cuda.cufft_plan_cache.size`` gives the number of plans\n  currently residing in the cache.\n\n* ``torch.backends.cuda.cufft_plan_cache.clear()`` clears the cache.\n\nTo control and query plan caches of a non-default device, you can index the\n``torch.backends.cuda.cufft_plan_cache`` object with either a :class:`torch.device`\nobject or a device index, and access one of the above attributes. E.g., to set\nthe capacity of the cache for device ``1``, one can write\n``torch.backends.cuda.cufft_plan_cache[1].max_size = 10``.\n\n.. _cuda-just-in-time-compilation:\n\nJust-in-Time Compilation\n------------------------\n\nPyTorch just-in-time compiles some operations, like torch.special.zeta, when\nperformed on CUDA tensors. This compilation can be time consuming\n(up to a few seconds depending on your hardware and software)\nand may occur multiple times for a single operator since many PyTorch operators actually\nselect from a variety of kernels, each of which must be compiled once, depending on their input.\nThis compilation occurs once per process, or just once if a kernel cache is used.\n\nBy default, PyTorch creates a kernel cache in $XDG_CACHE_HOME/torch/kernels if\nXDG_CACHE_HOME is defined and $HOME/.cache/torch/kernels if it's not (except on Windows,\nwhere the kernel cache is not yet supported). The caching behavior can be directly\ncontrolled with two environment variables. If USE_PYTORCH_KERNEL_CACHE is set to 0 then no\ncache will be used, and if PYTORCH_KERNEL_CACHE_PATH is set then that path will be used\nas a kernel cache instead of the default location.\n\nBest practices\n--------------\n\nDevice-agnostic code\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nDue to the structure of PyTorch, you may need to explicitly write\ndevice-agnostic (CPU or GPU) code; an example may be creatin\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 56000:70383]\n    print(y_cpu)\n\n        tensor([[ 0.3000,  0.3000],\n                [ 0.3000,  0.3000],\n                [ 0.3000,  0.3000]])\n\n    y_gpu = x_gpu.new_full([3, 2], fill_value=-5)\n    print(y_gpu)\n\n        tensor([[-5.0000, -5.0000],\n                [-5.0000, -5.0000],\n                [-5.0000, -5.0000]], device='cuda:0')\n\n    y_cpu_long = x_cpu_long.new_tensor([[1, 2, 3]])\n    print(y_cpu_long)\n\n        tensor([[ 1,  2,  3]])\n\n\nIf you want to create a tensor of the same type and size of another tensor, and\nfill it with either ones or zeros, :meth:`~torch.ones_like` or\n:meth:`~torch.zeros_like` are provided as convenient helper functions (which\nalso preserve :class:`torch.device` and :class:`torch.dtype` of a Tensor).\n\n::\n\n    x_cpu = torch.empty(2, 3)\n    x_gpu = torch.empty(2, 3)\n\n    y_cpu = torch.ones_like(x_cpu)\n    y_gpu = torch.zeros_like(x_gpu)\n\n\n.. _cuda-memory-pinning:\n\nUse pinned memory buffers\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\n.. warning::\n\n    This is an advanced tip. If you overuse pinned memory, it can cause serious\n    problems when running low on RAM, and you should be aware that pinning is\n    often an expensive operation.\n\nHost to GPU copies are much faster when they originate from pinned (page-locked)\nmemory. CPU tensors and storages expose a :meth:`~torch.Tensor.pin_memory`\nmethod, that returns a copy of the object, with data put in a pinned region.\n\nAlso, once you pin a tensor or storage, you can use asynchronous GPU copies.\nJust pass an additional ``non_blocking=True`` argument to a\n:meth:`~torch.Tensor.to` or a :meth:`~torch.Tensor.cuda` call. This can be used\nto overlap data transfers with computation.\n\nYou can make the :class:`~torch.utils.data.DataLoader` return batches placed in\npinned memory by passing ``pin_memory=True`` to its constructor.\n\n.. _cuda-nn-ddp-instead:\n\nUse nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel instead of multiprocessing or nn.DataParallel\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nMost use cases involving batched inputs and multiple GPUs should default to\nusing :class:`~torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel` to utilize more\nthan one GPU.\n\nThere are significant caveats to using CUDA models with\n:mod:`~torch.multiprocessing`; unless care is taken to meet the data handling\nrequirements exactly, it is likely that your program will have incorrect or\nundefined behavior.\n\nIt is recommended to use :class:`~torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel`,\ninstead of :class:`~torch.nn.DataParallel` to do multi-GPU training, even if\nthere is only a single node.\n\nThe difference between :class:`~torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel` and\n:class:`~torch.nn.DataParallel` is: :class:`~torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel`\nuses multiprocessing where a process is created for each GPU, while\n:class:`~torch.nn.DataParallel` uses multithreading. By using multiprocessing,\neach GPU has its dedicated process, this avoids the performance overhead caused\nby GIL of Python interpreter.\n\nIf you use :class:`~torch.nn.parallel.DistributedDataParallel`, you could use\n`torch.distributed.launch` utility to launch your program, see :ref:`distributed-launch`.\n\n.. _cudaGetDeviceCount:\n    https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-runtime-api/group__CUDART__DEVICE.html#group__CUDART__DEVICE_1g18808e54893cfcaafefeab31a73cc55f\n\n.. _cuInit:\n    https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-driver-api/group__CUDA__INITIALIZE.html#group__CUDA__INITIALIZE_1g0a2f1517e1bd8502c7194c3a8c134bc3\n\n.. _nvmlDeviceGetCount_v2:\n    https://docs.nvidia.com/deploy/nvml-api/group__nvmlDeviceQueries.html#group__nvmlDeviceQueries_1ga93623b195bff04bbe3490ca33c8a42d\n\n.. _cuda-graph-semantics:\n\nCUDA Graphs\n-----------\n\nA CUDA graph is a record of the work (mostly kernels and their arguments) that a\nCUDA stream and its dependent streams perform.\nFor general principles and details on the underlying CUDA API, see\n`Getting Started with CUDA Graphs`_ and the\n`Graphs section`_ of the CUDA C Programming Guide.\n\nPyTorch supports the construction of CUDA graphs using `stream capture`_, which puts a\nCUDA stream in *capture mode*. CUDA work issued to a capturing stream doesn't actually\nrun on the GPU. Instead, the work is recorded in a graph.\n\nAfter capture, the graph can be *launched* to run the GPU work as many times as needed.\nEach replay runs the same kernels with the same arguments. For pointer arguments this\nmeans the same memory addresses are used.\nBy filling input memory with new data (e.g., from a new batch) before each replay,\nyou can rerun the same work on new data.\n\nWhy CUDA Graphs?\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nReplaying a graph sacrifices the dynamic flexibility of typical eager execution in exchange for\n**greatly reduced CPU overhead**. A graph's arguments and kernels are fixed, so a graph replay\nskips all layers of argument setup and kernel dispatch, including Python, C++, and CUDA driver\noverheads. Under the hood, a replay submits the entire graph's work to the GPU with\na single call to `cudaGraphLaunch`_.  Kernels in a replay also execute slightly faster\non the GPU, but eliding CPU overhead is the main benefit.\n\nYou should try CUDA graphs if all or part of your network is graph-safe (usually this means\nstatic shapes and static control flow, but see the other :ref:`constraints<capture-constraints>`)\nand you suspect its runtime is at least somewhat CPU-limited.\n\n.. _Getting Started with CUDA Graphs:\n    https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/cuda-graphs/\n.. _Graphs section:\n    https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-c-programming-guide/index.html#cuda-graphs\n.. _stream capture:\n    https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-c-programming-guide/index.html#creating-a-graph-using-stream-capture\n.. _cudaGraphLaunch:\n    https://docs.nvidia.com/cuda/cuda-runtime-api/group__CUDART__GRAPH.html#group__CUDART__GRAPH_1g1accfe1da0c605a577c22d9751a09597\n.. _conditional nodes:\n    https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/dynamic-control-flow-in-cuda-graphs-with-conditional-nodes/\n\nPyTorch API\n^^^^^^^^^^^\n\n.. warning::\n    This API is in beta and may change in future releases.\n\nPyTorch exposes graphs via a raw :class:`torch.cuda.CUDAGraph` class\nand two convenience wrappers,\n:class:`torch.cuda.graph` and\n:class:`torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables`.\n\n:class:`torch.cuda.graph` is a simple, versatile context manager that\ncaptures CUDA work in its context.\nBefore capture, warm up the workload to be captured by running\na few eager iterations. Warmup must occur on a side stream.\nBecause the graph reads from and writes to the same memory addresses in every\nreplay, you must maintain long-lived references to tensors that hold\ninput and output data during capture.\nTo run the graph on new input data, copy new data to the capture's input tensor(s),\nreplay the graph, then read the new output from the capture's output tensor(s).\nExample::\n\n    g = torch.cuda.CUDAGraph()\n\n    # Placeholder input used for capture\n    static_input = torch.empty((5,), device=\"cuda\")\n\n    # Warmup before capture\n    s = torch.cuda.Stream()\n    s.wait_stream(torch.cuda.current_stream())\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        for _ in range(3):\n            static_output = static_input * 2\n    torch.cuda.current_stream().wait_stream(s)\n\n    # Captures the graph\n    # To allow capture, automatically sets a side stream as the current stream in the context\n    with torch.cuda.graph(g):\n        static_output = static_input * 2\n\n    # Fills the graph's input memory with new data to compute on\n    static_input.copy_(torch.full((5,), 3, device=\"cuda\"))\n    g.replay()\n    # static_output holds the results\n    print(static_output)  # full of 3 * 2 = 6\n\n    # Fills the graph's input memory with more data to compute on\n    static_input.copy_(torch.full((5,), 4, device=\"cuda\"))\n    g.replay()\n    print(static_output)  # full of 4 * 2 = 8\n\nSee\n:ref:`Whole-network capture<whole-network-capture>`,\n:ref:`Usage with torch.cuda.amp<graphs-with-amp>`, and\n:ref:`Usage with multiple streams<multistream-capture>`\nfor realistic and advanced patterns.\n\n:class:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` is more sophisticated.\n:class:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` accepts Python functions and\n:class:`torch.nn.Module`\\s. For each passed function or Module,\nit creates separate graphs of the forward-pass and backward-pass work. See\n:ref:`Partial-network capture<partial-network-capture>`.\n\n.. _capture-constraints:\n\nConstraints\n~~~~~~~~~~~\n\nA set of ops is *capturable* if it doesn't violate any of the following constraints.\n\nConstraints apply to all work in a\n:class:`torch.cuda.graph` context and all work in the forward and backward passes\nof any callable you pass to :func:`torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables`.\n\nViolating any of these will likely cause a runtime error:\n\n* Capture must occur on a non-default stream. (This is only a concern if you use the raw\n  :meth:`CUDAGraph.capture_begin<torch.cuda.CUDAGraph.capture_begin>` and\n  :meth:`CUDAGraph.capture_end<torch.cuda.CUDAGraph.capture_end>` calls.\n  :class:`~torch.cuda.graph` and\n  :func:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` set a side stream for you.)\n* Ops that synchronize the CPU with the GPU (e.g., ``.item()`` calls) are prohibited.\n* CUDA RNG operations are permitted, and when using multiple :class:`torch.Generator` instances within a graph,\n  they must be registered using :meth:`CUDAGraph.register_generator_state<torch.cuda.CUDAGraph.register_generator_state>` before graph capture.\n  Avoid using :meth:`Generator.get_state<torch.get_state>` and :meth:`Generator.set_state<torch.set_state>` during capture;\n  instead, utilize :meth:`Generator.graphsafe_set_state<torch.Generator.graphsafe_set_state>` and :meth:`Generator.graphsafe_get_state<torch.Generator.graphsafe_get_state>`\n  for managing generator states safely within the graph context. This ensures proper RNG operation and generator management within CUDA graphs.\n* Dynamic control flow (based on CPU or GPU data) is prohibited, unless it is based on GPU data and implemented via higher order operator\n  torch.cond(). See :ref:`Data Dependent Control Flow<graph-data-dependent-control-flow>`.\n\n\n\nViolating any of these will likely cause silent numerical errors or undefined behavior:\n\n* Within a process, only one capture may be underway at a time.\n* No non-captured CUDA work may run in this process (on any thread) while capture is underway.\n* CPU work is not captured. If the captured ops include CPU work, that work will be elided during replay.\n* Every replay reads from and writes to the same (virtual) memory addresses.\n* Dynamic shapes are prohibited. The graph assumes every tensor in the captured op sequence\n  has the same size and layout in every replay.\n* Using multiple streams in a capture is allowed, but there are :ref:`restrictions<multistream-capture>`.\n\nNon-constraints\n~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\n* Once captured, the graph may be replayed on any stream.\n\n.. _whole-network-capture:\n\nWhole-network capture\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nIf your entire network is capturable, you can capture and replay an entire iteration::\n\n    N, D_in, H, D_out = 640, 4096, 2048, 1024\n    model = torch.nn.Sequential(torch.nn.Linear(D_in, H),\n                                torch.nn.Dropout(p=0.2),\n                                torch.nn.Linear(H, D_out),\n                                torch.nn.Dropout(p=0.1)).cuda()\n    loss_fn = torch.nn.MSELoss()\n    optimizer = torch.optim.SGD(model.parameters(), lr=0.1)\n\n    # Placeholders used for capture\n    static_input = torch.randn(N, D_in, device='cuda')\n    static_target = torch.randn(N, D_out, device='cuda')\n\n    # warmup\n    # Uses static_input and static_target here for convenience,\n    # but in a real setting, because the warmup includes optimizer.step()\n    # you must use a few batches of real data.\n    s = torch.cuda.Stream()\n    s.wait_stream(torch.cuda.current_stream())\n    with torch.cuda.stream(s):\n        for i in range(3):\n            optimizer.zero_grad(set_to_none=True)\n            y_pred = model(static_input)\n            loss = loss_fn(y_pred, static_target)\n            loss.backward()\n            optimizer.step()\n    torch.cuda.current_stream().wait_stream(s)\n\n    # capture\n    g = torch.cuda.CUDAGraph()\n    # Sets grads to None before capture, so backward() will create\n    # .grad attributes with allocations from the graph's private pool\n    optimizer.zero_grad(set_to_none=True)\n    with torch.cuda.graph(g):\n        static_y_pred = model(static_input)\n        static_loss = loss_fn(static_y_pred, static_target)\n        static_loss.backward()\n        optimizer.step()\n\n    real_inputs = [torch.rand_like(static_input) for _ in range(10)]\n    real_targets = [torch.rand_like(static_target) for _ in range(10)]\n\n    for data, target in zip(real_inputs, real_targets):\n        # Fills the graph's input memory with new data to compute on\n        static_input.copy_(data)\n        static_target.copy_(target)\n        # replay() includes forward, backward, and step.\n        # You don't even need to call optimizer.zero_grad() between iterations\n        # because the captured backward refills static .grad tensors in place.\n        g.replay()\n        # Params have been updated. static_y_pred, static_loss, and .grad\n        # attributes hold values from computing on this iteration's data.\n\n.. _partial-network-capture:\n\nPartial-network capture\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nIf some of your network is unsafe to capture (e.g., due to dynamic control flow,\ndynamic shapes, CPU syncs, or essential CPU-side logic), you can run the unsafe\npart(s) eagerly and use :func:`torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` to graph only\nthe capture-safe part(s).\n\nBy default, callables returned by :func:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables`\nare autograd-aware, and can be used in the training loop as direct replacements\nfor the functions or :class:`nn.Module<torch.nn.Module>`\\ s you passed.\n\n:func:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` internally creates\n:class:`~torch.cuda.CUDAGraph` objects, runs warmup iterations, and maintains\nstatic inputs and outputs as needed.  Therefore (unlike with\n:class:`torch.cuda.graph`) you don't need to handle those manually.\n\nIn the following example, data-dependent dynamic control flow means the\nnetwork isn't capturable end-to-end, but\n:func:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables`\nlets us capture and run graph-safe sections as graphs regardless::\n\n    N, D_in, H, D_out = 640, 4096, 2048, 1024\n\n    module1 = to\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 76000:80000]\nt 11 DDP-enabled eager iterations before capture.\n\n.. _graph-memory-management:\n\nGraph memory management\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nA captured graph acts on the same virtual addresses every time it replays.\nIf PyTorch frees the memory, a later replay can hit an illegal memory access.\nIf PyTorch reassigns the memory to new tensors, the replay can corrupt the values\nseen by those tensors.  Therefore, the virtual addresses used by the graph must be\nreserved for the graph across replays. The PyTorch caching allocator achieves this\nby detecting when capture is underway and satisfying the capture's allocations\nfrom a graph-private memory pool. The private pool stays alive until its\n:class:`~torch.cuda.CUDAGraph` object and all tensors created during capture\ngo out of scope.\n\nPrivate pools are maintained automatically. By default, the allocator creates a\nseparate private pool for each capture. If you capture multiple graphs,\nthis conservative approach ensures graph replays never corrupt each other's values,\nbut sometimes needlessly wastes memory.\n\nSharing memory across captures\n~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\nTo economize the memory stashed in private pools, :class:`torch.cuda.graph`\nand :func:`torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` optionally allow different\ncaptures to share the same private pool.\nIt's safe for a set of graphs to share a private pool if you know they'll always\nbe replayed in the same order they were captured,\nand never be replayed concurrently.\n\n:class:`torch.cuda.graph`'s ``pool`` argument is a hint to use a particular private pool,\nand can be used to share memory across graphs as shown::\n\n    g1 = torch.cuda.CUDAGraph()\n    g2 = torch.cuda.CUDAGraph()\n\n    # (create static inputs for g1 and g2, run warmups of their workloads...)\n\n    # Captures g1\n    with torch.cuda.graph(g1):\n        static_out_1 = g1_workload(static_in_1)\n\n    # Captures g2, hinting that g2 may share a memory pool with g1\n    with torch.cuda.graph(g2, pool=g1.pool()):\n        static_out_2 = g2_workload(static_in_2)\n\n    static_in_1.copy_(real_data_1)\n    static_in_2.copy_(real_data_2)\n    g1.replay()\n    g2.replay()\n\nIt's also safe to share a memory pool across separate graphs that do not depend\non each other's outputs, provided they never run concurrently.\nBe aware that replaying one graph can clobber another graph's outputs when\nthey share a pool, unless :meth:`~torch.Tensor.clone` is called on the outputs\nbeforehand.\nThis pattern is frequently used in inference servers that accept variable batch\nsizes at runtime.\nvLLM is a notable example; see `here <https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/938a81692ea318e59ead4750e7e7425bfd6a4896/vllm/platforms/interface.py#L508-L515>`__\nand `here <https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/blob/938a81692ea318e59ead4750e7e7425bfd6a4896/vllm/compilation/cuda_graph.py#L86-L89>`__.\n\nWith :func:`torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables`, if you want to graph several\ncallables and you know they'll always run in the same order (and never concurrently)\npass them as a tuple in the same order they'll run in the live workload, and\n:func:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` will capture their graphs using a shared\nprivate pool.\n\nIf, in the live workload, your callables will run in an order that occasionally changes,\nor if they'll run concurrently, passing them as a tuple to a single invocation of\n:func:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` is not allowed. Instead, you must call\n:func:`~torch.cuda.make_graphed_callables` separately for each one.\n\n.. _graph-data-dependent-control-flow:\n\nData Dependent Control Flow\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nData-dependent control flow can with cuda graphs if the control flow\nis implemented using torch.cond(). If your function uses this\nfunction, compiling it with the \"cudagraphs\" backend will enable\ncontrol flow in the resulting cuda graph via `conditional nodes`_.\n\nDoing cuda graph capture of eager mode code using torch.cond() will\nalso work.\n\nSupport for inductor backend to torch.compile is not avail", "excerpt_ranges": [[16000, 20000], [28000, 36000], [40000, 44000], [48000, 52000], [56000, 70383], [76000, 80000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 80851, "selected_characters": 38383, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-PAGED/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "1f7c1718e253614838ba3e453297a8e222867506b737c3d3dedec2e3b026cc40", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 0:12000]\n--- PAGE 1 ---\nEfficient Memory Management for Large Language\nModel Serving with PagedAttention\nWoosuk Kwon1,∗Zhuohan Li1,∗Siyuan Zhuang1 Ying Sheng1,2 Lianmin Zheng1 Cody Hao Yu3\nJoseph E. Gonzalez1 Hao Zhang4 Ion Stoica1\n1UC Berkeley\n2Stanford University\n3Independent Researcher\n4UC San Diego\nAbstract\nHigh throughput serving of large language models (LLMs)\nrequires batching sufficiently many requests at a time. How-\never, existing systems struggle because the key-value cache\n(KV cache) memory for each request is huge and grows\nand shrinks dynamically. When managed inefficiently, this\nmemory can be significantly wasted by fragmentation and\nredundant duplication, limiting the batch size. To address\nthis problem, we propose PagedAttention, an attention al-\ngorithm inspired by the classical virtual memory and pag-\ning techniques in operating systems. On top of it, we build\nvLLM, an LLM serving system that achieves (1) near-zero\nwaste in KV cache memory and (2) flexible sharing of KV\ncache within and across requests to further reduce mem-\nory usage. Our evaluations show that vLLM improves the\nthroughput of popular LLMs by 2-4× with the same level\nof latency compared to the state-of-the-art systems, such\nas FasterTransformer and Orca. The improvement is more\npronounced with longer sequences, larger models, and more\ncomplex decoding algorithms. vLLM’s source code is publicly\navailable at https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm.\n1\nIntroduction\nThe emergence of large language models (LLMs) like GPT [5,\n37] and PaLM [9] have enabled new applications such as pro-\ngramming assistants [6, 18] and universal chatbots [19, 35]\nthat are starting to profoundly impact our work and daily\nroutines. Many cloud companies [34, 44] are racing to pro-\nvide these applications as hosted services. However, running\nthese applications is very expensive, requiring a large num-\nber of hardware accelerators such as GPUs. According to\nrecent estimates, processing an LLM request can be 10× more\nexpensive than a traditional keyword query [43]. Given these\nhigh costs, increasing the throughput—and hence reducing\nPermission to make digital or hard copies of part or all of this work for\npersonal or classroom use is granted without fee provided that copies are\nnot made or distributed for profit or commercial advantage and that copies\nbear this notice and the full citation on the first page.
